# Addresses to cripple rather than drop.
#
# THE SPLIT WITH custom-ip-blocklist.txt: VPNs and tunnels come here and are
# shaped; app estates — imo, BIGO/Likee, TikTok Live, the live-streaming and
# random-chat backends, DNS-changers — are dropped there. The reasoning is in the
# paragraph below: a dropped app backend just fails, where a dropped VPN node tells
# the client to try the next one. That file's header carries the same rule.
#
# Everything here is capped at 100 kbit/s each way, instead of being blocked
# outright. The point is that a tunnel behaves like a bad connection rather than
# a filtered one: there is no clean failure for the client to detect and route
# around, and an app that reconnects instantly when a node is dropped has
# nothing to react to when the node merely becomes slow.
#
# THE CAP IS NOW THE WHOLE OF IT — changed 2026-08-13. This file used to add
# 400 ms of latency, 100 ms of jitter and 3% random loss on top, and the old
# text here argued the jitter mattered more than the bitrate. Watching it work
# says otherwise: the clients being shaped choose between candidate nodes by
# RTT and loss, so impairing a node made it trivially easy for them to spot,
# discard and go hunting for an unthrottled one. The impairment was advertising
# which nodes had been touched.
#
# A bare rate cap does not show up in that scoring. A latency probe is a couple
# of small packets and passes under the cap untouched, so the node keeps
# measuring healthy and keeps being selected, while anything carrying real
# traffic crawls. The client staying on a node that cannot move data is the
# whole objective; a node it can tell is bad is a node it will replace.
#
# The queue under the cap is fq_codel, not a plain fifo, and that is part of the
# same design — a deep fifo at 100 kbit/s is minutes of standing buffer, which
# an RTT probe reads exactly the way it used to read netem's delay. See the
# comment on the throttled class in qos.nix.
#
# Same format and same guarantees as custom-ip-blocklist.txt: parsed at build
# time, so a typo fails the rebuild rather than silently shaping nothing, and
# overlapping entries are collapsed. Add live with `tempthrottle add <ip>`,
# which is cleared by the next rebuild — anything worth keeping belongs here.
#
# TEST SITE: https://ash-speed.hetzner.com/ — Hetzner's Ashburn speed test.
# Present so the shaping can be verified end to end from a LAN client: run the
# speed test and the result should be ~100 kbit/s. Latency should now look
# ORDINARY — that is the point of the 2026-08-13 change, not a sign the shaping
# has stopped working, so judge it on throughput alone. A ping to this address
# staying fast while the transfer crawls is the shaper behaving correctly.
# Remove it once the throttle is confirmed working.
5.161.7.195

# that pair of braces, since a zone whose whole purpose is minting new names
# for other people's backends can mint one this list has never seen.
#
# A /32 and not the /23: CDS is a general-purpose cloud, so the neighbours are
# other people's tenants.

# Fifth in the CDS Global Cloud group, 148.153.167.0/24, AS63199. Added 2026-08-12
# from the same rotation round as the block at the end of this file. Same /32
# reasoning as above.
#
# CORRECTION, same day: this note first said it was filed here "because the AS and
# the operator are the ones this group already documents". The AS is right, the
# operator is not — there is no single operator here. 148.153.0.0/16 is CDS Global
# Cloud / capitalonline shared hosting, and the addresses above it belong to at
# least two unrelated things: 148.153.116.134 is what lifegram.cc CNAMEs to, while
# 38.123.110.196, 148.153.50.179, 148.153.67.208 and 148.153.234.111 are Top VPN
# exit nodes, identified by decrypting that app's own node list (see the
# topvpn.cc section in custom-ip-blocklist.txt). Sharing a /16 of general-purpose
# cloud says nothing about sharing an operator — the same point this file makes
# against every CDN and cloud range in it.
#
# So this address is a /32 on the strength of having been handed over during the
# round, and nothing more. Do not read the neighbours as attribution.
148.153.167.45

# block goes rather than the single address — exit nodes rotate within a provider's
# range. The ports in custom-port-blocklist.txt are the durable form of this; the
# range is here for the case where a client finds a transport those do not cover.
#

# being moved rather than by the range question being reopened. The standing

# pool and is untouched by it — do not cite this line as precedent for taking a
# range there.

# A full-device tunnel, caught 2026-08-01 in a netwatch window. Different
# operator from the four above, which are all AS63199: this is AS394381
# (L7-CONNECT - L7 Connect, US) in 154.51.185.0/24.
#
# It took 97.1% of the device's bytes — 90.9 MB of a 93.6 MB window, with only
# 4450 bytes going anywhere else at all, and the peer count collapsing from 112
# the window before to 19. That is not an app with a dominant peer, it is the
# whole device routed through one address.
#
# Every frame was UDP on port 2311, 73 MB down against 17 MB up, with 53106
# packets at over 1400 bytes and 3116 IP fragments — bulk transfer at MTU,
# which a voice or video call does not produce. No DNS has ever resolved into
# 154.51.185.0/24, there was no TLS and so no SNI, and no certificate could be
# retrieved, so there is no name anywhere to attribute it by. Deliberately not
# confused with the allowed VoIP relays, which resolve normally under their own
# domains and sit in entirely different ranges.
#
# A /32 rather than the /24: the /24 has no reverse DNS on the address or its
# neighbours and nothing has ever resolved into it, which is suggestive of a
# single tenant but is three lookups rather than proof. Expect the operator to
# move — a tunnel endpoint is the easiest thing in this file to replace.
154.51.185.190
# SailfishX tunnel nodes — see *.sailfishx.live in custom-blocklist.txt for what
# the service is. DigitalOcean droplets reached with no DNS lookup and no SNI,
# which is the whole tell: a handset has no other reason to hold a raw session
# with a rented VPS. Observed carrying:
#
#   157.245.194.245  tcp/553  the tunnel proper — 264 MB in one ~1h session
#   143.198.214.26   tcp/22   SSH, version banner then steady payload
#   138.68.74.158    udp/443  full-MTU 1282/1287 both ways, WireGuard-shaped
#
# None of the three serves TLS on 443, so there is no certificate to examine and
# no co-tenant visible to damage. DigitalOcean is shared cloud, so /32s rather
# than ranges, consistent with the rest of this file. Expect the list to rotate:
# these are droplets and a fresh one costs the operator nothing, so the name
# block above is the durable control and these are the addresses seen in use.
157.245.194.245

# one relocating.

# stacksnippets.net (a Stack Overflow property) is 198.252.206.1. So the name is
# camouflage chosen to look ordinary in a TLS log, not a CNAME or a shared host,
# and it cannot be reached by any entry in custom-blocklist.txt. Ace Data Centers
# / IPXO leased space.

# tcp/22, also unexplained by DNS. AltusHost B.V. (AS51430, NL). SSH carrying the
# whole of a phone's traffic is a SOCKS tunnel, not administration.

# The same tool as the two entries above, caught properly this time. Watching a
# device across four successive drops showed it is not a single rented host but
# a client carrying a node pool, and the two /32s above were simply the members
# that happened to be in use that day.
#
# It moves between four transports and will switch mid-session when one is
# dropped: SSH on tcp/22 (its default), raw UDP on a random high port, TLS to an
# SNI proxy that forwards on whatever name it is handed, and a cleartext HTTP
# channel that carries the payload base64-encoded in a "Cookie: rvs=..." header
# with Content-Type: text/xml and no User-Agent. It also fakes its SSH identity
# — one handset announced itself as PuTTY_Release_0.68, and the same device sent
# two different OpenSSH version strings to two nodes minutes apart, with the
# KEXINIT algorithm order shuffled each time. Authentication is a password to
# root@, which is how these are sold: a monthly SSH account plus a config file.
#
# The fronting names it presents are in custom-blocklist.txt, but that list is
# nearly decorative here — the client dials every one of these by address and
# was never seen resolving any of them. Addresses are the only thing that bites,
# and they are a rearguard action: each drop produced a replacement from a
# different provider within about a minute, so treat this block as raising the
# cost rather than closing the door. Grouped by host below only to show the
# spread; there is no single operator to take down.
#
# DigitalOcean:

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# NOT EVERYTHING THROTTLED IS IN THIS FILE. One path is handled by a live rule
# that deliberately has no entry here, because the address it targets must not
# be in an address list at all.
#
# The tunnel operator is a paying Akamai customer: their domain has a real
# certificate provisioned on Akamai edge, so the client speaks ordinary HTTPS to
# a CDN address and Akamai forwards to their origin. The names are sinkholed in
# custom-blocklist.txt and the client reaches the edge anyway on a cached
# address, so the name layer does not stop it.
#
# The edge addresses cannot go in this file. 95.100.170.0/24 is shared Akamai
# edge — the same addresses serve Apple's iTunes certificate and TikTok assets
# to every other device on the LAN. Throttling them here would degrade those for
# the whole house. The client also moves between edge addresses freely
# (.42 -> .49 -> .51 observed), so pinning single addresses does not hold.
#
# So it is scoped to the client instead, as a live rule. Reapply after a reboot
# — this survives `nixos-rebuild switch` but not a restart, and nothing will
# indicate it is missing except the traffic going fast again:
#
#   ssh <router> 'sudo -n nft delete table inet flow_throttle 2>/dev/null;
#                 sudo -n /run/current-system/sw/bin/nft -f -' <<'RULE'
#   table inet flow_throttle {
#     chain shape {
#       type filter hook forward priority 1; policy accept;
#       ip saddr 192.168.50.220 ip daddr 95.100.170.0/24 counter meta mark set 0x2
#       ip saddr 95.100.170.0/24 ip daddr 192.168.50.220 counter meta mark set 0x2
#       ip saddr 192.168.50.156 ip daddr 95.100.170.0/24 counter meta mark set 0x2
#       ip saddr 95.100.170.0/24 ip daddr 192.168.50.156 counter meta mark set 0x2
#       ip saddr 192.168.50.219 ip daddr 95.100.170.0/24 counter meta mark set 0x2
#       ip saddr 95.100.170.0/24 ip daddr 192.168.50.219 counter meta mark set 0x2
#     }
#   }
#   RULE
#
# 192.168.50.219 added 2026-08-12 on capture evidence — see the Psiphon-meek note
# further down against 95.100.170.58. That handset ran 2462 POSTs to / on port 80
# across .57 and .59, 52 MB in about seven minutes. NOT YET APPLIED on the router:
# the rule above is the recommendation, and the routers were unreachable when it
# was written.
#
# Deliberately whole-/24 and both ports rather than `tcp dport 80`. Narrowing to 80
# would leave the handset normal HTTPS access to Akamai content, which is kinder to
# the device, but meek runs over 443 too and a clean failure on 80 is an invitation
# to move there. Shaping everything to the /24 leaves no working path to switch to,
# which is the same reasoning as the rest of this file.
#
# Delete the table first: `nft -f` appends to an existing chain rather than
# replacing it, so re-running without the delete silently doubles the rules.
# Mark 0x2 is what the tc filters in qos.nix steer into the throttled class, so
# this gets the same treatment as everything listed above.
#
# The cost, which is accepted deliberately: those two clients lose Akamai-hosted
# content generally, not only the tunnel. No other client is affected.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
159.203.16.60
64.23.232.164

# as RTSP but the payload is opaque, the device resolved plenty of unrelated

# Same client again, about twenty minutes later, and the entry that should be
# read before trusting any port-based rule in this file: it left OVH entirely

# prompting a reconnect somewhere faster. Three addresses across two providers
# in thirty-five minutes is the point at which address-by-address stops
# converging; a per-device egress policy is the lever that does not depend on
# guessing the next address, and port 22 is still absent from
# custom-port-blocklist.txt. Recorded here because the decision was to keep
# looping, and the record is what makes the next round legible.

15.224.47.11

# A second address in 45.78.192.0/18, the APNIC allocation 45.78.196.212 above
# came from. Both were live on the same handset in one capture, on several
# simultaneous ports each — the older one mixing TCP and UDP as its note
# records, this one TCP on 6651, 51590 and 3135 — near-symmetric at keepalive
# rate, no resolver line for either and no TLS, so no SNI or certificate to
# attribute them by. Two /24s of one /18 on one device at one moment is the
# range repeating within a single round rather than across days, which is
# tighter than anything else in this file and worth raising if a third appears.
# Kept as a /32: the allocation is APNIC-direct with no netname behind it, so
# what else lives in it is unknown rather than known-innocent.
#
# Read alongside the operational note that came out of the same round: every
# address in this file was inert at this site when the round started. A plain
# `systemctl reload nftables` re-applies the base ruleset, which declares
# throttle4, imo4 and local_block4 empty, and nft-blocklists-local is wired
# `partOf` nftables.service — which propagates stop and restart but *not*
# reload, so nothing repopulates them. remote_block4 survives because
# nft-blocklists-restore is ordered after nftables and runs again. The window
# is silent: traffic simply goes fast and no unit is failed. So an address
# listed here still carrying traffic is not automatically a shaping failure or
# a client defeating the shaper — check the set is populated before concluding
# anything about the client.

# OVH Sp. z o. o., OVH-DEDICATED-FO, 51.75.42.0/26 — "Failover IPs", a LEGACY
# inetnum registered PL, and a third OVH inetnum for the same client that the
# 51.75.168.2 and 51.75.39.84 entries above already record in this /16.
#
# The entry worth reading for the transport rather than the address: this is the
# cleartext-HTTP mode the note above 199.244.102.198 warns a port-based rule will
# miss. It is a single established session on tcp/80 that tshark dissects as real
# HTTP, so nothing about the port or the protocol looks out of place. What gives
# it away is the content of the requests — POST / with `Host:` set to the bare
# address rather than any name, `Content-Type: image/jpeg` alongside
# `Content-Length: 0` so no JPEG is ever actually sent, the payload carried in a
# `Cookie:` header instead of a body, and every response coming back as chunked
# application/octet-stream. Several hundred identical requests to `/`, roughly
# eight to one inbound at about 570 kbit/s, which is a tunnel carrying browsing.
#
# It also camouflages against a casual check, which is the part worth recording:
# probed directly it answers tcp/80 with `Server: AkamaiGHost` and a 400 for any
# Host header, so a whois-plus-banner glance reads it as CDN edge. It is not —
# neighbouring addresses in the same /26 answer as ordinary Apache or not at all,
# so the banner is specific to this host rather than something on the path. tcp/22
# and tcp/443 accept connections but never present a banner or complete a TLS
# handshake even with SNI, which is a listener waiting for its own client.
# No name has ever resolved to it on either site's resolver.
#
# AS16276 now holds twelve addresses in this file across several inetnums, far
# more than any other hoster here, and the four-address note further up is stale
# at that count. An AS-wide rule is worth putting to the user again on that basis
# alone; kept as a /32 for now, consistent with the standing decision.
51.75.42.29

# A fan-out, and the one block in this round whose evidence is a different kind
# from everything above it. Read the distinction before treating these as
# equivalent rows.
#
# Twelve DigitalOcean addresses seen in a single view, every one with byte counts
# identical to the decimal — 272 B out, 92 B in — all on tcp/20107, all at 0.0%
# of the device. That is not traffic. 272 bytes is a connection opened and
# nothing sent through it, repeated across a dozen hosts on one port.
#
# So the justification here is NOT the whole-of-device share that every entry
# above rests on; none of these carried anything. It is that they are the pool: a
# client fanning out to twelve rented hosts on one uncommon port, in one moment,
# is enumerating candidate nodes, and the pool is what this file has been trying
# to reach address by address all evening. Shaping a node before it is used is
# the only version of this that gets ahead of the rotation rather than following
# it.
#
# The weakness is the mirror of that strength, and belongs on the record: a
# fan-out of bare handshakes is consistent with a node list, but a 272-byte probe
# carries no payload to characterise, no direction ratio, no TLS and so no SNI.
# Nothing in the flows themselves distinguishes these from any other software
# that sweeps a port across a provider. What ties them to this client is the
# moment they appeared in and the fact that tcp/20107 matches nothing else on the
# network — circumstantial, and weaker per-address than the entries above, though
# the uniformity across twelve hosts is hard to read another way.
#
# If any of these later turns up carrying a real session, say so against it: that
# would convert the whole block from inference to observation, and would also be
# the first direct evidence anywhere in this file of how the client picks its
# next node.
#
# All twelve are AS14061 and all are /32s, consistent with the rest of the file.
#
# ANSWERED, AND THE TWELVE HAVE MOVED TO custom-ip-blocklist.txt. The note above
# asked to be told if any of these ever turned out to be more than a bare
# handshake. All twelve are DoH relays: every one serves a cloudflare-dns.com
# certificate on 443 and every one answers a real DNS-over-HTTPS query. They were
# not candidate tunnel nodes being enumerated, they were a resolver pool, and the
# tcp/20107 fan-out was a client checking which members were alive.
#
# The reasoning above is left in place rather than deleted because it was honest
# about its own weakness — "nothing in the flows themselves distinguishes these"
# — and that caveat is exactly what turned out to matter. The inference that they
# were one pool was right; the inference about what kind was wrong, and no amount
# of looking harder at 272-byte handshakes would have settled it. Probing the
# addresses did, in about a second each.
# Was "second in 146.190.0.0/16, alongside the .132.199 in the fan-out above" —
# that companion is now in custom-ip-blocklist.txt as a DoH relay, so this is the
# only 146.190 address left from that view. It came in on its own rather than as
# part of the fan-out, and it does NOT answer DoH or serve a resolver
# certificate, so it stays here. Whether it shares the probe shape or was
# carrying something is still not recorded.
146.190.31.189

# IONOS, inetnum 217.160.159.0/24, netname de-rhr-bap-ngcs-public — the sixteenth
# 217.160 address in this file, and the one that settles what all of them are.
#
# The netname was checked across the group rather than assumed, which overturned
# the guess it was added on. de-rhr-bap-ngcs-public is NOT specific to the recent
# additions: 217.160.34.78, 217.160.33.174 and 217.160.104.239 carry it too, and
# 217.160.155.154 and 217.160.102.24 carry es-lgr-pl-ngcs-public. So every 217.160
# address this client has ever used is IONOS NGCS public-IP space — Next Generation
# Cloud Server, their cloud-instance product — split across two sites, a German one
# and a Spanish one.
#
# That is worth more than another row, because it reframes the /24 counting in the
# older notes. The entry above 217.160.34.49 makes much of "three /24s each reaching
# three occupants" and calls it "the tightest repeat pattern of any hoster in this
# round". Those /24s are slices of one product's address pool at one datacentre, so
# repeats inside them are what drawing repeatedly from that pool looks like, not
# evidence of anything narrower. The count is real; the tightness it was read as is
# not.
#
# It also means IONOS is a two-site relationship rather than a two-continent one:
# the 74.208.x US space is a different product again, and the DE/ES split here is
# a customer picking regions in one console. Nothing wider is takeable — NGCS public
# pools are shared by every IONOS cloud customer in that region, which is the same
# reasoning that keeps DigitalOcean and Hetzner at /32 throughout this file.
217.160.159.101
#
# Hetzner, AS24940, inetnum 178.104.224.0/20, netname CLOUD-NBG1 — 178.104.0.0/15
# is new to this file. Checked the same way as the IONOS group immediately above,
# because that check overturned an assumption; this one confirms a better fact:
#
#   37.27.15.151     37.27.0.0/20      CLOUD-HEL1
#   37.27.91.243     37.27.80.0/20     CLOUD-HEL1
#   89.167.12.196    89.167.0.0/20     CLOUD-HEL1
#   46.224.91.79     46.224.80.0/20    CLOUD-NBG1
#   46.225.132.30    46.225.128.0/20   CLOUD-NBG1
#   116.203.96.227   116.203.96.0/19   CLOUD-NBG1
#   178.104.236.173  178.104.224.0/20  CLOUD-NBG1   <- this one
#   188.245.69.44    188.245.0.0/16    DE-HETZNER-20110117
#   162.55.0.240     162.55.0.0/16     DE-HETZNER-19920803
#
# Every Hetzner address this client has used in this round is HETZNER CLOUD, in two
# regions — Helsinki and Nuremberg. The two exceptions are older entries from other
# investigations: 162.55.0.240 is the proxy.dlmate.cc host near the top of this
# file, and 188.245.69.44 predates this round. So the "six Hetzner ranges in play"
# the note above 37.27.25.51 counts are not scattered ranges at all; they are one
# product's public-IP pools at two datacentres.
#
# READ WITH THE IONOS NOTE ABOVE, because together they say something no single
# address does. At IONOS every address is NGCS public space across two sites; at
# Hetzner every address is Cloud across two sites. This client rents CLOUD
# INSTANCES — API-provisioned, billed by the hour, destroyed and recreated in
# seconds — rather than dedicated servers or leased ranges. That is a mechanism for
# the rotation rate this whole round has been chasing: nodes are not being found and
# replaced, they are being minted on demand, and the supply is bounded by the
# provider's regional pool rather than by anything the operator holds.
#
# The practical consequence is the conclusion the 159.203.1.196 note already reached
# on different evidence, now with a mechanism under it: no quantity of /32s reaches
# a supply this elastic, and the ranges cannot be taken because they are shared with
# every other cloud customer in the region. It is also why the AltusHost group reads
# differently — leased space with configured PTRs is something held, not minted.
#
# Kept from being overread: this says how the client BUYS, not who it is, and it
# does not distinguish this tenant from any other Hetzner Cloud customer. It is an
# argument about which lever works, not evidence of attribution.

46.166.172.173
#
# PSIPHON. These two are a different kind of entry from everything else in this
# block and should not be read with the rest of it.
#
# Both are consumer broadband rather than rented hosting, which is why they were
# held back and queried rather than added with the batch:
#
#   1.54.217.114   AS18403 FPT Telecom, VN.  netname FPTDYNAMICIP-NET,
#                  status ASSIGNED NON-PORTABLE — an explicitly DYNAMIC pool.
#   65.18.190.45   AS15600 Quickline AG, CH. netname FTTH-NET-GAW, "FTTH
#                  Network", Nidau — fibre to the home.
#
# They were confirmed as Psiphon nodes from a capture, which makes them the ONLY
# addresses in this whole round with a characterised flow behind them. Everything
# else here rests on "handed over during the round" plus registration arithmetic.
# Weight them accordingly, and weight the rest accordingly too.
#
# Psiphon uses residential and volunteer exits alongside rented servers, so a
# subscriber address is expected here rather than anomalous — the usual objection
# in this file, that consumer space means a call or an innocent tenant, does not
# apply once the tool is identified. Note the inverse though: the GoChat entry
# further up establishes that on at least one handset a symmetric flow to a
# subscriber address IS a call to leave alone. Both readings are live on this
# network, and only a capture separates them, which is exactly what these two had
# and the rest of the round did not.
#
# THE DYNAMIC-POOL CAVEAT, which is the real cost of these two entries.
# FPTDYNAMICIP-NET reassigns: 1.54.217.114 belongs to a different household on a
# different day, and when it does, this file will be shaping an unrelated Vietnamese
# subscriber with nothing in the entry to connect the shaping back to a decision.
# That is a failure mode no other address here has — a rented VPS stays rented to
# whoever rented it. If these are still in the file weeks from now, re-verify them
# rather than trusting the row; a stale dynamic entry is worse than no entry.
#
# WHAT THIS IMPLIES FOR THE REST OF THE ROUND, offered as a hypothesis to test and
# NOT as an attribution of the other entries. Psiphon rotates aggressively across
# exactly the providers this round has been chasing — DigitalOcean, OVH, Hetzner,
# Scaleway, Linode, Oracle, AltusHost — and distributes fresh server lists to its
# clients out of band, by design, specifically so that blocking servers one at a
# time does not converge. That is a precise description of what this file has spent
# the round doing. If the wider rotation is also Psiphon, then no amount of /32s or
# even AS-wide rules reaches it, and the honest lever is a per-device egress policy,
# which the note above 159.203.1.196 already reached for on different grounds.
#
# Do not fold the rest of the round into this on the strength of the shape alone.
# The named-PTR evidence points elsewhere: infogral.is and the base64 .buzz zones
# are somebody's own naming, and Psiphon does not name its servers. The likeliest
# reading is more than one tool in play, which is what the SailfishX and imo entries
# above already establish for this network.
1.54.217.114
65.18.190.45
# A third Psiphon node, 2026-08-12, and the most perishable entry in this file.
#
# AS197207 MCCI — Mobile Communication Company of Iran, Hamrah-e Aval — inetnum
# 113.203.64.0/18, netname IR-MCI-20081128, prefix 113.203.96.0/20. That is not
# fixed-line consumer space like the two above: it is a MOBILE CARRIER, so the
# address is behind carrier NAT and fronts many subscribers at once, and it is
# reassigned on a timescale of hours rather than days.
#
# The dynamic-pool caveat written against 1.54.217.114 therefore applies here
# several times over. The mitigating fact, and the reason it is still worth adding,
# is that the collateral is bounded by direction: this file only shapes traffic
# between this house and that address, so an unrelated MCCI subscriber inheriting
# it is only affected if somebody here also talks to them — which for an Iranian
# mobile address is realistically nothing. That is the opposite of the Akamai case
# refused above, where the shared host is one the whole house reaches constantly.
# Cheap to hold, but re-verify rather than trusting the row.
#
# Iran being a principal Psiphon market, a mobile-carrier exit there is the
# expected shape rather than a surprise, and it strengthens the Psiphon reading of
# the three together: fixed-line VN, fibre CH and mobile IR is a residential exit
# pool spread across three countries and three access technologies, which is what
# the tool is built on and is nothing like the rented-VPS rotation in the rest of
# this block.
#
# One methodological note in this address's favour: here the repo's
# ip2asn-combined.tsv and live RIPE data AGREE, both giving AS197207 MCCI. Recorded
# because the 154.17.253.112 entry above documents that table returning a confident
# wrong answer, and it would be unfair to leave it looking useless — it is right
# most of the time and wrong without warning, which is why announcements get checked
# rather than the table trusted.

# Third in 185.10.58.0/24, and the address that made the reverse-DNS sweep below
# worth running: its PTR is ytewotm1njq5.sacas.buzz. That is not a hoster default
# — AltusHost's own addresses resolve to swe-net-ip / nld-net-ip / rs-network-ip

#
# 185.10.58.188, already in this file, carries the same construction:
# zgywodawyzqw.cloudpabxip.buzz. Both labels are twelve characters, both zones are
# .buzz, and both labels are base64. DNS discards the original capitalisation, so
# the casing was brute-forced over all 4096 possibilities; exactly one decoded to
# printable text in each case:
#
#   ytewotm1njq5  =  base64("a10935649")   on 185.10.58.142
#   zgywodawyzqw  =  base64("df0800c40")   on 185.10.58.188
#
# Nine characters each, both hex-shaped. That is an identifier scheme rather than a
# hostname — an account or instance number encoded so it does not read as one.
#
# Neither sacas.buzz nor cloudpabxip.buzz resolves forward at all: no A, no NS, no
# SOA. So these names exist only as reverse records the customer set on AltusHost,
# pointing at zones that were never published. "cloudpabxip" reads as cloud-PABX-IP,
# telephony-flavoured, which is the kind of plausible-sounding label the
# 144.225.91.11 entry near the top of this file already documents this client using
# — that one fabricated status-29.shop-51.stacksnippets.net as an SNI.
#
# This is the best correlator AltusHost has produced, and it does not settle
# attribution on its own: a shared encoding across two addresses in one /24 says
# one customer configured both, not that the customer is this client. What it gives
# is a test costing one lookup — an address whose PTR base64-decodes to a short
# hex-shaped identifier under a non-resolving zone is the same tenant as these two.
185.10.58.142

# Hetzner, AS24940, 116.203.0.0/16 — and the only address in this round that is
# worth more than its row, because it is the first one all evening with a PTR

# Every other reverse name tonight identifies a product — instances.scw.cloud,
# clients.your-server.de, as51430.net. This one is a zone somebody configured, and
# the zone enumerates a pool by name, which is the thing this file has been
# chasing address by address since 17:55:
#
#   node-1   78.47.56.71        Hetzner AS24940, 78.46.0.0/15
#   node-2   78.47.168.188      Hetzner
#   node-3   116.203.96.227     Hetzner   <- the address handed over here
#   node-4   116.203.244.102    Hetzner
#   node-5   65.21.153.100      Hetzner
#   node-6   95.216.245.44      Hetzner
#   node-7   144.21.62.15       Oracle Cloud AS31898
#   node-8   141.147.87.80      Oracle
#   node-9   132.145.67.92      Oracle
#   node-10  143.47.227.106     Oracle
#   node-11  144.21.60.43       Oracle
#   node-12  132.226.129.228    Oracle
#
# node-13 and upward do not resolve, so twelve is the whole of it. Six Hetzner and
# six Oracle, which is exactly the supply pattern the Oracle note above 144.24.170.223
# predicted — always-free instances alongside rented ones. The zone's own NS records
# are node-1 through node-4, so the operator runs its DNS on the pool itself.
#
# WHAT THIS IS NOT, YET. The apex is a live WordPress site presenting as
# "infogralis | Competitive Intelligence and Market Insights", on Hetzner
# 144.76.221.59, with www and mail on the same address and a joke response header
# (all_your_base: is_belong_to_us). A market-intelligence firm running a scraper
# fleet across cheap providers would look EXACTLY like this — twelve numbered nodes
# on Hetzner and Oracle is unremarkable for that business. So the pool being real
# does not make it this client's.
#
# The single thing tying it here is that 116.203.96.227 was handed over as a node
# the client had moved to, and per the caveat at the top of this block that came
# with no capture behind it. Everything else is inference from a naming scheme.
# node-3 refuses tcp/443 outright, so it is not serving the website, but "not a web
# server" is a long way from "tunnel node".
#
# ONLY node-3 is shaped. Taking all twelve plus *.infogral.is in
# custom-blocklist.txt was put to the user and declined; node-3 alone stands.
# That is the right call on the evidence as it is — adding the other eleven would
# convert one weakly-attributed address into eleven, and if the zone belongs to a
# legitimate crawler operator that is eleven unrelated hosts shaped on the strength
# of a PTR.
#
# What would settle it is a capture: one flow to node-3 with a share and a
# direction ratio, the way every entry above this block was justified. If that
# comes back as a tunnel, the other eleven are already mapped and the zone block
# becomes worth having; if it does not, delete this note and the entry with it.
#
# Either way the mapping is the durable part of this entry. It is the only time in
# the whole round that a node came with a name behind it rather than a number, so
# even if this particular pool is innocent, the method is the one thing tonight
# that got ahead of the rotation instead of following it: check the PTR on every
# address handed over, because a configured zone enumerates in one query what
# address-by-address shaping spent four hours failing to enumerate.
116.203.96.227

# REVERSE-DNS SWEEP of every address in this file, run once the .buzz naming above

#   79.142.74.43     mail-sor-g38.kingtropin.com.br
#   107.182.236.121  wadsguide.org.uk             ** WITHDRAWN, see below **
#
# CORRECTION, 2026-08-12. The fourth of those is not an operator-configured name and
# should never have been listed beside the other three. When 107.182.236.27 came in
# with PTR thansmart.org.uk — same /24, same two-word .org.uk construction — the
# whole /24 was swept, and it is midphase.com shared hosting: the unassigned
# addresses all resolve to <ip>.static.midphase.com, and the assigned ones are
# hundreds of unrelated tenants. Bulk mailers (mta*.dailyshipandclick.com,
# vmta*.frackthestack.com, emsg*.bayesiantrader.org), and dozens of the same
# throwaway-domain pattern in families — stall*/stallon* (10 names), than* (12),
# naafi*/naffing* (10), wads* (8), bicyclic* (7), weeds* (6) across .org.uk, .co.uk,
# .store, .site, .pw, .wiki, .club.
#
# That is an SEO/PBN domain farm on a shared-hosting block, not one operator naming
# its nodes. wadsguide.org.uk is one of sixty identical-looking names, and the fact
# that it does not resolve forward — which is what made it look like the .buzz zones
# — is unremarkable for a lapsed PBN domain.
#
# The other three stand: infogral.is enumerates twelve nodes on two clouds, the two
# .buzz labels decode to a shared identifier scheme, and kingtropin.com.br grafts
# Google's SMTP naming onto an unrelated domain. The sweep's conclusion is now
# THREE schemes, all three on hosts the client rented alone, and none on shared
# hosting. If anything that tightens it.
#
# The third is worth more than its line. 79.142.74.43 is the OLDEST AltusHost
# address in this file — the 2026-08-05 tcp/22 tunnel that took 100% of a handset
# — and mail-sor-g38 is Google's outbound SMTP naming (mail-sor-fNN.google.com)
# grafted onto an unrelated Brazilian domain. That is the same fabrication habit as
# the stacksnippets.net SNI and the .buzz zones: a name chosen to look ordinary in
# a log. Neither kingtropin.com.br nor wadsguide.org.uk resolves forward, exactly
# like the two .buzz zones.
#
# So there is no single zone enumerating this pool. What the sweep did establish is
# that four separate operator-configured naming schemes appear across it, all four
# pointing at zones that do not resolve, and three of the four on AltusHost. That
# is consistent with the "held rather than rented ad hoc" reading of AltusHost
# above, and it is still short of proof.

# DELIBERATELY NOT SHAPED, recorded so the same address is not handed over twice:

# Shaping a CDN edge degrades whatever ordinary sites the house reaches through
# that POP, for everyone, which is the same self-harm the imo notes in
# custom-ip-blocklist.txt refuse for Etisalat-hosted Akamai. A client can dial an
# Akamai address for entirely innocent reasons — that is what a CDN is — so an
# address appearing in a round is not by itself attribution.
#
# The general rule this round has now tested twice: check the PTR and the origin
# AS BEFORE adding, not after. Every other address tonight came back as rented VPS
# space where a /32 lands on one tenant; this one did not.
#
# AND IT HAPPENED AGAIN. 95.100.170.58 was handed over on 2026-08-12, eight
# addresses along from .50 in the same /24, and was refused on the same grounds:
# AS20940, prefix 95.100.170.0/24, the block whose .10, .100 and .200 all resolve
# to deploy.static.akamaitechnologies.com. Not added, not shaped.
#
# Two Akamai addresses out of one /24 in one round is worth more than the two rows
# it did not become. Whatever is being watched keeps reaching this POP, which is
# what a device does when ordinary traffic — app updates, media, any site fronted
# by Akamai — flows through the nearest edge. An address appearing in a capture
# means the device talked to it, not that the thing under investigation did, and
# a CDN POP is where that distinction bites hardest.
#
# If a THIRD address from 95.100.170.0/24 comes up, the useful response is not a
# third refusal: it is to find out which flows are actually landing there, because
# by then the capture method is producing Akamai edge repeatedly and that is a
# property of the method rather than of the client.
#
# CORRECTED 2026-08-12 by a capture, and the reasoning above was wrong in the way
# that matters. 192.168.50.219-20260812-1324.pcap shows 95.100.170.59:80 carrying
# 45 MB in 315 seconds — 41 MB down against 3.4 MB up — and 95.100.170.57:80
# carrying 7.3 MB more, from ONE handset. Those are not incidental CDN fetches.
# They are the tunnel. The speculation above that "an address appearing in a
# capture means the device talked to it, not that the thing under investigation
# did" is exactly backwards for this /24.
#
# Not adding the addresses to this file was still right, and for the reason the
# section at the top of this file already gives rather than the one invented here:
# 95.100.170.0/24 is shared Akamai edge serving the whole house, so it must not go
# in an address list at all. The remedy was already written up there too — a live
# per-device rule marking that client's traffic to the /24 into the throttle class.
# It covers 192.168.50.220 and 192.168.50.156 and now needs 192.168.50.219.
#
# The real error was not consulting that section before answering twice. It
# documents this precise path — paying Akamai customer, sinkholed names reached by
# cached address anyway, edge addresses unusable as a list entry — and had it been
# read, the answer would have been "add the device to the flow_throttle rule"
# rather than "this is CDN noise".
#
# WHAT THE CAPTURE ADDS to that section, which predates it. The transport is
# Psiphon meek, in the open on port 80 rather than the HTTPS the section describes:
# 2462 requests, all POST / , all with
#
#   Host: www.shoppeninegreen.com
#   Content-Type: application/octet-stream
#   Cookie: D=nAPOvAUClnR46XCg2g
#   Accept-Encoding: gzip
#
# and no User-Agent at all; responses 200 with Content-Type application/octet-stream
# on a keep-alive connection. The Cookie is the meek session ID and the POST body is
# the tunnel payload.
#
# It is domain fronting, and the DNS proves it: the only names resolved in the whole
# capture are a1262.b.akamai.net and www.google.com. www.shoppeninegreen.com is
# NEVER looked up — and it has no A record anywhere, apex or www. The client
# resolves a generic Akamai edge name, connects to it on 80, and hands Akamai a Host
# header for a property that does not exist in public DNS. Akamai maps the Host to
# the customer's configuration regardless.
#
# Consequences for anyone reaching for a name-based fix, all of them negative:
#   * Blocking www.shoppeninegreen.com in custom-blocklist.txt does NOTHING. It is
#     never queried and does not resolve.
#   * a1262.b.akamai.net IS queried and does resolve — to .57 and .59, confirmed
#     live. But aNNNN.b.akamai.net is a shared Akamai map serial, not this
#     customer's own name, so blocking it takes out whatever unrelated properties
#     share serial 1262. Testable, not obviously safe.
#   * Blocking tcp/80 to Akamai wholesale is the wrong shape twice over: it hits
#     every device for a protocol Akamai mostly uses for HTTPS redirects, and meek
#     runs over 443 just as happily, so a clean failure buys a move to a port that
#     cannot be touched without breaking the house. That is this file's founding
#     argument applied to a port instead of an address.

# ===========================================================================
# Sixteen addresses handed over on 2026-08-12, from 15:06 through the evening,
# and the provenance has to be read before the rows are: THERE IS NO CAPTURE
# BEHIND THESE. Every other block in this file cites a share, a transport and a
# direction because a capture produced it. These arrived as a list, were desk-
# checked, and were written straight here. They were deliberately NOT applied
# with `tempthrottle` — no live device was touched — so they take effect at the
# next rebuild and not before.
#
# A later reader must not count these sixteen as sixteen observations. What is
# actually established about each one is that it is a rented instance where a
# /32 lands on a single tenant, which is the safety question, not the
# attribution question. Whether any given row was carrying a tunnel is on the
# word of whoever handed it over.
#
# THE CHECK APPLIED TO ALL SIXTEEN, which is the 95.100.170.x rule from further
# up finally run as routine rather than after the fact: PTR, inetnum and netname,
# origin AS via Team Cymru, and a TLS probe on 443. All sixteen came back as
# general-purpose cloud — Scaleway instance PTRs under instances.scw.cloud, DO-13
# and DIGITALOCEAN netnames, one Hetzner clients.your-server.de — and none is CDN
# edge. Nothing was refused this round, unlike the two Akamai addresses above.
#
# WHAT THE TLS PROBE FOUND, which is the one genuinely new thing here and does
# not depend on trusting the handover. Five of the sixteen answer on 443, and
# none of them serves a certificate of its own:
#
#   51.158.234.37    a248.e.akamai.net              (Akamai, chain verifies)
#   51.158.178.205   a248.e.akamai.net              (Akamai, chain verifies)
#   129.212.153.150  akamai.download.microsoft.com  (Microsoft, chain verifies)
#   167.172.2.72     swdist.apple.com               (Apple EV, chain verifies)
#
# A DigitalOcean droplet does not hold Apple's or Microsoft's key, so these are
# forwarding the handshake to the real origin rather than terminating it. That is
# the "TLS to an SNI proxy that forwards on whatever name it is handed" transport
# the long note further up describes, caught from the outside for the first time
# instead of inferred from a capture. The two DigitalOcean hosts forward to one
# fixed upstream whatever SNI they are given and simply fail on an unknown name;
# the two Scaleway hosts are the general case — hand 51.158.234.37 the name
# example.com and it returns example.com's certificate.
#
# The detail worth keeping, because it ties the two Scaleway hosts to one
# operator rather than to a coincidence of software: handed example.com they both
# return the IDENTICAL certificate, serial 0AD893BA…, valid Jan 15 2025 to Jan 15
# 2026 — expired, and not the live one (serial 0624D0AB…, issued Jul 2026). Two
# independent hosts serving the same stale chain for a name neither owns means a
# shared certificate store baked into a shared image. It also means the fronting
# is sloppier than it looks: an expired chain fails verification in any client
# that checks, so this mode survives inspection only until someone looks.
#
# All three upstreams are Akamai-fronted properties, which is consistent with the
# Psiphon-meek finding above rather than separate from it.
#
# OVERLAPS WITH THE BLOCK BELOW THIS ONE, which was written into the file while
# this one was being composed and covers a much larger sweep of the same evening.
# Three of the sixteen — 51.15.120.52, 51.158.234.37 and 46.101.64.89 — appeared
# in both. They are listed HERE, and the block below has dropped them from its
# lists (its headers read 33 and 18 for that reason).
#
# WORTH KNOWING ABOUT, because it nearly cost three addresses: both blocks were
# deduplicated against each other at the same moment, each removing the rows it
# thought the other was keeping, and for a few minutes all three were in the file
# zero times. Caught by re-checking presence after the edit rather than assuming
# it. If this file is ever edited from two places at once again, verify each
# address still resolves to exactly one row afterwards — the generator collapses
# genuine duplicates harmlessly (`net in seen`), so a repeat is cheap and a
# mutual deletion is silent.
#
# Every count in this block is as of writing and was recomputed against the file,
# not carried over: the concurrent block moved most of them within the hour. If
# you are about to quote one, recount.
#
# Grouped by hoster below rather than in handover order, since the order carries
# nothing without captures to hang on it.
#

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# SSH-tunnel app: one rotation, 73 nodes
#
# A single capture, and every address below came out of it. This is the largest
# batch this file has taken at once, and the reason to take it as a batch is that
# the rotation was observed end to end rather than inferred: node used, node
# shaped, next node from a different provider within seconds, repeatedly, for the
# length of the capture. Blocking one at a time is what produced the previous
# ~30 entries; this is the rest of the list those were being drawn from.
#
# IDENTIFICATION. The transport is plain SSH, and the client says so — SSH-2.0
# banners on tcp/22 to a dozen of these. What names the app is that the CLIENT
# banner is different on almost every connection:
#
#   PuTTY_Release_0.68  0.69  0.70  0.71  0.72  0.74  0.77
#   OpenSSH_8.0  8.2  8.2p1  8.6  8.7  8.7p1  8.8
#
# A real SSH client has one version string. Fourteen, cycling per connection from
# one device, is deliberate fingerprint randomisation, and it is the house style
# of the HTTP-Injector / HTTP-Custom / SocksIP family of tunnel apps — the ones
# that ship an importable "config" file containing a server list and a payload.
# That is also why the server list is this long and this diverse: it came with the
# config, and the app walks it on failure.
#
# THREE TRANSPORTS, ONE LIST. The same server list is reached three ways, which
# matters because a port-based rule catches at most one of them:
#
#   * SSH on tcp/22, unobfuscated.
#   * Opaque high-entropy TCP on 80, 179, 443, 553, 554 and random high ports.
#     Nothing on those ports parses: no TLS record header, no HTTP. tcp/179 is
#     BGP and tcp/553 and 554 are not in normal client use at all — the port is
#     chosen to survive a filter, not to mean anything.
#   * Spoofed Host / SNI. Requests carry names like
#         www.getmyspiritshutterday.com   rxmightyblogger.org
#         www.wellnessgeniusextrabuilder.com   thingspackmorefabric.net
#     none of which is ever resolved in the capture and most of which have no A
#     record. These are the app's "payload" strings — a fake Host header to get
#     past name-based DPI. Blocking them by name in custom-blocklist.txt does
#     nothing, for exactly the reason recorded in the meek section above: a name
#     that is never queried cannot be denied at the resolver.
#
# WHY /32s AND NOT THE RANGES. 33 DigitalOcean addresses across 20-odd /16s and
# 18 Scaleway across four discontiguous blocks is the provider's allocation, not
# a tenancy. Same argument as the Hetzner table earlier in this file: the spread
# proves rented instances, and rented instances mean the neighbours are innocent.
#
# WHAT WAS DELIBERATELY LEFT OUT, so it does not get "fixed" later:
#   * Akamai. Part of this rotation fronts through Akamai edges, with the same
#     fake Host headers, and it was the fallback the client reached for as the
#     rest of the list degraded. It is not listed and must not be — see the meek
#     analysis above, which is the same technique against the same CDN.
#   * Residential and mobile exits (AT&T, SFR, Iran MCCI, Syrian Telecom, Uganda
#     NITA, Superloop AU, Ethio Telecom). Real addresses in the rotation, but they
#     are other people's connections and they rotate; pinning them ages badly.
#     The hosted relays they were reached through ARE listed.
#   * imo/PageBites, TikTok/Bytedance, CDN77, Google, Facebook. Present in the
#     capture, unrelated to the tunnel. imo belongs in custom-ip-blocklist.txt
#     under this file's founding split, not here.
#
# ON THE WEBRTC-SHAPED NODES. Some of this rotation negotiates STUN against
# stun.l.google.com and then runs DTLS, which reads as a voice call at first
# glance and is meant to. It is not one: there are four separate DTLS sessions to
# four unrelated networks on three continents, sequential, none overlapping, each
# lasting under two minutes, interleaved with the SSH hopping on the same
# timeline. A call negotiates once and keeps media on one peer. This is the same
# rotation wearing a call's clothes, and the hosted endpoints of it are listed
# below alongside the rest — 46.225.22.118 and 23.251.50.166 in particular, which
# would otherwise look like innocent TURN relays.
#
# stun.l.google.com is untouchable for the same reason Akamai is: it is the
# discovery mechanism for every legitimate call in the house.

# 174.138.100.118, handed over in the same run as the twenty DoH relays that are
# now in custom-ip-blocklist.txt, but NOT one of them — it stayed here because it
# is a tunnel node rather than a resolver, and that check is the only reason it
# did not get dropped with them. Nothing on 443 at all: no certificate, no TLS,
# and a DoH request to it fails outright.
#
# What it does have is the signature already written up against 51.75.42.29
# further up: tcp/22 is the only open port, it accepts the connection, and it
# then sends NOTHING. A real SSH server emits its version banner the moment the
# socket opens — that is the first thing in the protocol — so a silent listener
# on 22 is waiting for a client that already knows what to say. The rotation
# documented above forges SSH banners rather than suppressing them, so this is
# the quieter variant of the same idea.
#
# DigitalOcean AS14061, 174.138.100.0/22, and 174.138.96.149 from the rotation
# block above is the other 174.138 address here. /32, as ever.

151.115.67.40
#
# --- Handed over as a bare address with no capture behind it; everything below
# is a desk check, not observed traffic.
#
# 151.115.78.178 — Scaleway SCALEWAY-WAW, AS12876, PTR
# 178-78-115-151.instances.scw.cloud, so a /32 on one tenant. Only tcp/443 is
# open — 22, 80, 553, 554, 853 and 8080 all refuse — and it is not a resolver:
# the DoH probe returns nothing at all and 853 is closed, which is what keeps it
# out of the blocklist.
#
# It is a fronting proxy, and it relays rather than holds keys. With no SNI, and
# with a nonsense SNI (www.marriott.com), it answers with Akamai's
# a248.e.akamai.net certificate; hand it swdist.apple.com and it returns Apple's
# live swcdn.apple.com certificate instead. So unrecognised names fall through to
# a fixed Akamai upstream while recognised ones are passed through.
#
# The Akamai serial (0373AB420F54941B555742D9AC890626) is identical to what the
# real upstream 23.53.126.164 serves, and identical to what 151.115.72.41 —
# already in this file — serves. Per this file's usual caution that is same
# upstream, not same image: a pass-through returns whatever the upstream returns,
# so the match was expected and carries no weight on its own.
#
# The one result a pass-through does not explain: SNI example.com returns
# *.example.com, serial 0AD893BAFA68B0B7FB7A404F06ECAF9A, expired Jan 15 2026,
# while the live example.com certificate has since rotated (serial
# 0624D0AB311558780B7D5213B9631831, valid from Jul 29 2026). Repeated twice, same
# stale certificate both times. Something is being served locally for that name
# rather than relayed. CORROBORATED later in the same round: 159.203.50.29,
# added below, is a DigitalOcean droplet in a different country under a different
# AS, and it returns that identical expired certificate — same serial — for the
# same SNI, alongside the same Akamai default and the same live Apple
# pass-through. No pass-through explains two hosts on two providers serving one
# stale certificate for a name whose live certificate has rotated. They are
# serving from a shared store, i.e. one image. This is the fleet evidence the
# rest of the batch is read against.
#
# Sweeping the listed Scaleway neighbours for the same behaviour found three more
# fronting live: .45.183 serves www.apple.com, .72.41 serves a248.e.akamai.net,
# .74.253 serves akamai.download.microsoft.com. Same family, different default
# upstreams. Scanning Scaleway space for hosts answering with an upstream's
# certificate is how the rest of these get found in one pass instead of one at a
# time.
#
# Recomputed against this file: seventeenth 151.115 address, and fifteenth inside
# SCALEWAY-WAW's 151.115.0.0/17 (the other two, .146.148 and .150.240, sit in the
# separate 151.115.128.0/19 announcement). First in 151.115.78.0/24. Kept as a
# /32 — 151.115.0.0/17 is 32768 addresses of general-purpose Scaleway hosting and
# the neighbours are other people's tenants.

# ===========================================================================
# TWENTY-TWO ADDRESSES, 2026-08-12, handed over in four batches across one
# session. NO CAPTURE for any of them: none was observed in traffic here, none
# has a device, share or transport behind it. Everything below is a desk check —
# PTR, RIPE/ARIN whois, Team Cymru origin AS, TCP reachability on 22/80/443/853,
# TLS under several SNIs, and a DoH query with the body read. A later reader
# should not mistake any of it for capture evidence.
#
# TWO METHOD CORRECTIONS FIRST, because both were made and caught inside this
# batch and both would have put wrong words in the rows below.
#
# 1. PORT 53 CANNOT BE TESTED FROM HERE. This network blocks outbound 53, so a
#    UDP or TCP/53 probe times out for every address on earth and proves nothing
#    whatever. The first pass of this batch dutifully recorded "no answer on 53"
#    for fourteen addresses as if it were a finding; it was the local firewall
#    answering, not them. The resolver check that DOES work from here is DoH on
#    443 — a /dns-query request with SNI cloudflare-dns.com, reading the BODY.
#    Anything in this file that claims an address does not serve DNS on 53 is
#    unverified unless it was probed from somewhere else.
#
# 2. `openssl s_client -brief </dev/null` LIES ABOUT -servername. On these hosts
#    it printed no handshake at all for every named SNI while succeeding with
#    -noservername, which reads exactly like a server that drops named SNI. It
#    is an openssl artefact: the same connection through `curl -v --resolve`
#    completes and shows the certificate. The first pass of this batch described
#    five hosts as refusing named SNI on that basis, which was false. Use
#    `curl -sk -v --resolve name:443:$ip https://name/` as the probe of record
#    and treat openssl as a second opinion.
#
# WHAT THE CORRECTED PROBE SHOWS, and it is the most useful thing in this block:
# the Akamai-fronting hosts are pass-throughs to ONE AKAMAI EDGE, not general
# SNI proxies. The discriminator is a name Akamai does not host. Hand
# 151.115.67.213 or 138.68.200.111 an SNI Akamai serves and the genuine
# certificate comes back — example.com returns ICANN's *.example.com,
# swdist.apple.com returns Apple's swcdn.apple.com, both with the chain
# verifying. Hand either one www.wikipedia.org, github.com or www.google.com —
# none of them Akamai customers — and both fall back to a248.e.akamai.net. A
# general SNI proxy would have resolved those names and returned Wikipedia's and
# GitHub's real certificates. These do not: they have one fixed upstream, and it
# is an Akamai edge whose default certificate is a248.e.akamai.net.
#
# That reframes two things already written in this repo. The "returns a
# DIFFERENT certificate per SNI, therefore a general SNI proxy" reading in the
# entries above is not safe — one Akamai upstream produces exactly that. And the
# certificate tally at the end of custom-ip-blocklist.txt (69 a248.e.akamai.net,
# 10 www.apple.com, 7 swdist.apple.com, 6 akamai.download.microsoft.com, 5 each
# www.honda.com, starbucks.com, assets.adobedtm.com, 4 www.marriott.com) is not
# eight fronted brands; it is one upstream answering under whatever SNI the
# scanner happened to send. The rescan worth doing sends a NON-Akamai name and
# sorts hosts by whether they relay it or fall back.
#
# They are still relays and not Akamai: the origin AS is DigitalOcean, Scaleway,
# THG or Hetzner in every case, the PTRs are instance defaults, and Akamai does
# not deploy edge clusters into cloud tenant space. The refusal rule for real
# CDN edge — Akamai PTRs, AS20940/16625, something the whole house reaches —
# does not catch these. What cannot be settled from a desk check is whether each
# one is a circumvention relay or something duller pointed at a CDN, and the
# rows below say so rather than assuming.
#
# The resolver case was checked FIRST for all twenty-two, since that is the one
# belonging in the other file: not one returned DNS JSON to a /dns-query request,
# none presented a resolver certificate under SNI cloudflare-dns.com, and none
# has 853 open. So none is a DoH relay and all twenty-two belong here. One came
# close enough to be worth its own warning — 93.115.17.177.
#
# --- Five Akamai-edge pass-throughs on 443 ---------------------------------
#
# All five: TLS completes, the certificate tracks Akamai's hosting as described
# above, and a DoH request returns AkamaiGHost's 400 "Invalid URL" page rather
# than DNS.

# --- One Google-fronted relay, a form new to this file ---------------------
#
# 93.115.17.177 — MVPS LTD AS202448, Cyprus, 93.115.17.0/24, PTR
# ip-93-115-17-177-144706.vps.hosted-by-mvps.net. First 93.115 address here.
#
# tcp/22, tcp/80 and tcp/443. Port 443 is a pass-through to GOOGLE with a fixed
# upstream: example.com, cloudflare-dns.com, swdist.apple.com and a made-up
# nonsense-xyz.test all return CN=*.google.com with the chain verifying, and SNI
# www.google.com returns CN=www.google.com and HTTP 200. Note what that means
# next to the Akamai hosts above — example.com is an Akamai-hosted name, and
# this host does NOT return Akamai's certificate for it, so it is not resolving
# the SNI either. It has one upstream and that upstream is Google's frontend.
# No SNI at all gives Google's own "No SNI provided - please fix your client" /
# CN=invalid2.invalid.
#
# Worth its own paragraph because every fronted host in this file until now has
# pointed at Akamai. A second upstream provider for the same technique means the
# Akamai-shaped scan that produced the tally above was looking at one half of
# it; a rescan should count *.google.com and invalid2.invalid too.
#
# THE NEAR MISS, recorded so it is not repeated: a /dns-query request to this
# host with SNI cloudflare-dns.com returns HTTP 404 over a valid, verifying
# certificate. Status code alone reads as a resolver that happens to 404, and
# this would have gone to the blocklist. The body is Google's own "Error 404
# (Not Found)!!1" HTML — the relay forwards to Google, which has never heard of
# the path. Read the body, always. Second time that check has been the
# difference, after the FASTPANEL false positive in the other file.
#
# Port 80 serves an nginx/1.31.3 page titled "Backgammon", 20 KB of static HTML,
# Last-Modified 2026-08-11 — the day before this handover. A plausible-looking
# decoy landing page on a host whose 443 is a Google pass-through, put up the
# day before. Behaviour, not proof.
93.115.17.177

# THE WIKIPEDIA TEST SPLIT THE FRONTING HOSTS IN TWO, which the block above
# could not do because every fronted host it found behaved the same way. Handing
# each host a name Akamai does not serve now separates:
#
#   * SNI-FOLLOWING pass-through to an Akamai EDGE — example.com returns ICANN's
#     real certificate, www.wikipedia.org falls back to a248.e.akamai.net. The
#     upstream is a whole edge node serving many customers.
#   * FIXED-NAME upstream — one certificate for every SNI, whatever you ask for,
#     including wikipedia. The upstream is a single property, not an edge.
#
# Both are relays and both are shaped, but they are different configurations and
# an entry that does not say which one it saw is not reproducible. The
# fixed-name form also explains the brand tally in custom-ip-blocklist.txt more
# precisely than the block above does: hosts pinned to www.apple.com or
# assets.adobedtm.com produce exactly those counts no matter what SNI a scanner
# sends, so the tally mixes fixed-name hosts with edge pass-throughs and cannot
# be read as a preference for particular brands.
#

# --- The one row in this session with a real tunnel signature ---------------
#
# 88.208.225.105 — Fasthosts Internet Ltd, "UK Next Generation Cloud Server
# (NGCS)", inetnum 88.208.224.0 - 88.208.227.255, announced by AS8560 IONOS SE
# (Fasthosts is IONOS-owned, so the AS name and the inetnum name disagree
# without either being wrong). No PTR. tcp/22 and tcp/443.
#
# CORRECTED 2026-08-13: this row first said "first 88.208 address in this file
# and the first Fasthosts/IONOS host in it at all". Both were wrong.
# 88.208.240.76 — IONOS AS8560, opaque tcp/554 — has been in this file all
# along, on a line carrying a trailing comment, which the counting command used
# throughout that session silently skipped. See the note on that trap at the
# foot of the file. This is the SECOND 88.208 address and the second IONOS host.
#
# Everything else in this session fronts somebody else's certificate or says
# nothing at all. This one presents its OWN: CN=qnhyg.com, self-signed (issuer
# and subject both CN=qnhyg.com), valid 10 Apr 2026 to 10 Apr 2027, returned for
# every SNI including www.wikipedia.org. Behind it, every path returns an empty
# 404 with Content-Type text/plain and a zero-length body, over TLS 1.3 with
# X25519MLKEM768 — a current post-quantum-hybrid stack, so recent software.
#
# What makes it a signature rather than a curiosity: QNHYG.COM IS NOT A
# REGISTERED DOMAIN. VeriSign returns "No match", and it is NXDOMAIN in DNS. So
# no client can ever have reached this host by that name; the certificate exists
# only so that a handshake completes for a client that already knows the address
# and the name to send. Self-signed cert for a nonexistent domain, plus an empty
# 404 for anything unauthenticated, is the standard camouflage of a TLS-carried
# proxy (the trojan/VLESS family). That is the closest thing to a positive
# identification anywhere in these forty-seven rows, and it is still an
# inference from behaviour, not a capture.
#
# Worth reusing: an unregistered CN is cheap to test and hard to fake innocently.
# Any host in this file presenting a self-signed certificate is worth a whois on
# the name in it.
88.208.225.105
#

# should be read before trusting any "AkamaiGHost" row above. The genuine Akamai
# edge was fetched directly for comparison — 23.53.126.145, the live
# a248.e.akamai.net — and it returns THE IDENTICAL "Server: AkamaiGHost" 400
# Invalid URL page, same body, same length, as every relay in this session. So
# that response does NOT distinguish a relay from real CDN edge. It only says
# "an Akamai edge is on the other end of this", which is equally true of both.
#
# What actually distinguishes them, and what every row here rests on instead:
#
#   * THE NETWORK. DigitalOcean, Scaleway, THG, Hetzner and Fasthosts tenant
#     space is not where Akamai deploys edge clusters. The AS is the evidence.
#   * SSHD. The real edge has tcp/22 CLOSED. Every relay checked in this session
#     answers with an OpenSSH banner. An Akamai appliance does not offer you a
#     shell.
#
# That second test is what decided 205.237.95.199 below, which is the first
# address in this file where the refusal rule genuinely had to be weighed.
#
# A NEGATIVE RESULT, recorded so it is not re-derived: SSH banners were
# collected from thirty-odd hosts in this session to test whether the relays
# share a build. THEY DO NOT. Versions run 7.0 through 10.2 with no pattern —
# OpenSSH 7.0 and 8.4 both appear on fronting hosts, 7.2 and 10.2 both on hosts
# that front nothing. It looks like a pool of separately rented instances, not a
# fleet from one image. The banner is useful for telling a relay from a CDN
# appliance and useless for grouping the relays.
#

# separate sessions suggests a range the operator holds ... a wider block may be
# justified if a third address appears." THAT CONDITION WAS MET LONG AGO AND
# NOBODY RETURNED TO IT. Recounted against the file rather than trusting the
# note, this address makes EIGHT, all AS51430 AltusHost, across three of its
# sub-allocations:
#
#   79.142.74.0 - 79.142.75.255  (AS51430-NL)     .74.43, .75.12, .75.43, .75.56
#   79.142.76.0 - 79.142.77.238  (ALTUSHOST-NET)  .77.41, .76.179  <- this one
#   79.142.79.0/24               (CH-AH-NET)      .79.185, .79.195
#
# So the premise of that note is wrong in the direction that matters: it is not
# two addresses in two /24s, it is eight in three named allocations of one small
# hosting AS. That is a materially better range case than anything else in this
# file — better than 151.115.67.0/24 (six, but inside Scaleway's open instance
# pool) and better than the AS214623 case that was declined, because these are
# AltusHost's own sub-allocations rather than a slice of a general-purpose cloud.
#
# IT IS STILL NOT TAKEN HERE. AltusHost sells to other customers too, so a /23
# or wider rule shapes tenants who have nothing to do with this, and taking a
# range is the user's decision, not a later hand's. The three allocations above
# are what the decision would be over. Eight /32s until told otherwise, and the
# stale note has been marked in place so the next reader does not re-derive this.

# ===========================================================================
# A COUNTING TRAP IN THIS FILE, found 2026-08-13 and worth more than any single
# row above.
#
# EIGHT ADDRESSES IN THIS FILE ARE INVISIBLE TO THE OBVIOUS COUNTING COMMAND.
# They sit on lines of the form "<address>    # <comment>" — an address with a
# trailing comment on the same line — and every count taken during the 12-13 Aug
# session used:
#
#   sed 's/#.*//' custom-throttle-list.txt | grep -oE '^([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}$'
#
# which strips the comment but leaves the trailing whitespace, so the `$` anchor
# never matches and the line is dropped silently. The eight are 79.142.75.12,
# 46.225.22.118, 23.251.50.166, 150.241.249.34, 5.254.53.242, 88.208.240.76,
# 151.243.14.207 and 202.63.60.39.
#
# THE FIX is one more step — trim before matching:
#
#   sed 's/#.*//' custom-throttle-list.txt | sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' \
#     | grep -E '^([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}$'
#
# WHAT IT COST: three claims written during that session were wrong and have
# been corrected in place — "first 88.208 address and first IONOS host" (it was
# the second of each), "THIRD 46.225 address" (fourth), and the 79.142 recount
# (eight, not seven). The file total was quoted as 450 throughout that session
# and was in fact 458 before it started; the correct figures are 458 before and
# 566 after, with 108 added.
#
# Note the nft parser is NOT affected — it handles those lines correctly and all
# eight are genuinely shaped. This was only ever a counting error, never an
# operational one. But this file's notes lean on counts constantly ("nth address
# in this /22"), and a systematically low count in one direction is exactly the
# kind of thing that gets carried forward. Recompute with the trimmed form.
# ===========================================================================

# --- One that does not belong with the others, and is probably a mistake -----

# 81.0.219.99 — Contabo GmbH, AS51167, inetnum 81.0.218.0 - 81.0.219.255,
# netname TT-20221122, PTR vmi1103454.contaboserver.net. First Contabo address
# in this file. tcp/80 and tcp/443.
#
# IT MATCHES NO TUNNEL SIGNATURE HERE, and the evidence points the other way:
#
#   * Port 443 serves CN=api-staging.joiicare.com issued by LET'S ENCRYPT,
#     valid 14 Jul to 12 Oct 2026. An LE certificate requires proof of control
#     over the name at issuance, so whoever runs this box controlled that
#     subdomain — this is not a borrowed or relayed certificate.
#   * joiicare.com is a registered domain belonging to a real company, and
#     api-staging.joiicare.com resolves to Cloudflare rather than here, which is
#     what a company's public record looks like while a staging box sits on a
#     cheap VPS.
#   * Every path returns HTTP 200 with the 14-byte body "Not Authorized" — an
#     API refusing an unauthenticated caller, not a proxy.
#   * The same certificate comes back for example.com and www.wikipedia.org,
#     which reads like fronting until you notice it is ordinary nginx
#     default_server behaviour: one vhost, one certificate, served to anything.
#     Port 80 confirms the reading — the stock "Welcome to nginx!" page.
#
# So the honest description is a small company's staging API on a Contabo VPS.
# Added because the handover asked for it and the collateral is confined to one
# tenant, but it is the most likely false positive in this session, ahead of
# 192.124.26.34. If something in the house legitimately uses a Joii-branded app,
# this row will break it and should be removed first. What would settle it is
# which local device talked to it and in which direction — a capture, not a
# whois.
81.0.219.99

# ===========================================================================
# NINE MORE — batch fourteen, 2026-08-13, session total one hundred and
# thirty-one. Ten were handed over; 45.55.101.223 was already here.
#
# THE FIRST ROWS IN THIS SESSION WITH CAPTURE EVIDENCE. Everything above was
# desk check only. Eight of these nine were observed in packet captures taken on
# this network today, which settles what a hundred and twenty desk checks could
# only infer. What the captures show about the TOOL is recorded here once, since
# it explains the shape of this whole file and is worth more than any row.
#
# HOW THE CLIENT WORKS — candidates, then selection:
#
#   1. It holds a POOL OF CANDIDATE NODES and probes them in quick succession,
#      a handful of packets each, before committing.
#   2. It then SELECTS ONE and sends effectively everything through it. The
#      selected node carries the overwhelming majority of the traffic; the
#      candidates carry almost none. That is why this file fills up so fast
#      with addresses that look inert on a desk check: most entries here are
#      candidates that were probed and passed over, not nodes that carried
#      anything.
#   3. Selection is by measured quality. This is the direct evidence for the
#      2026-08-13 shaper change recorded at the head of this file — a node made
#      slow, laggy and lossy is a node this client discards at step 2, and it
#      simply picks another. The cap-only shaper exists so that a shaped node
#      still wins selection and then cannot move data.
#
# HOW IT HIDES — three techniques, all visible in the capture:
#
#   * RANDOMISED SNI. Each candidate is reached with a TLS/QUIC handshake
#     carrying a DIFFERENT invented domain name — plausible-looking but
#     unregistered strings, one per node, never reused. No name lookup precedes
#     them. Blocking by name is therefore pointless against this tool, and the
#     nonsense SNI is itself the tell: a genuine service is reached by a name
#     that resolves to it.
#   * PORT REUSE. Sessions were seen on tcp/22, tcp/21, tcp/80, tcp/443,
#     tcp/553, tcp/554, QUIC on 443 and on high ports, and UDP on high ports —
#     the same client using whichever port is likely to be open. Several of the
#     rows above that looked like a bare SSH listener or an FTP server on a desk
#     check are this: the port is camouflage, not a service.
#   * PORT 53 THAT IS NOT DNS. Several nodes are fed large opaque UDP datagrams
#     on port 53. A packet decoder labels anything on that port "DNS" and it is
#     nothing of the sort — real DNS in the same capture is a fraction of the
#     size, carries a readable query name and goes to this network's own
#     resolver. See the warning below, because this nearly caused eleven
#     addresses to be filed in the wrong file.
#
#   * A CONTROL CHANNEL THIS FILE CANNOT TOUCH. The client also reaches an
#     Akamai edge address carrying an invented SNI, and Akamai edge is on the
#     never-block list because the whole house depends on it. If that is how the
#     candidate pool is refreshed, then no amount of adding node addresses here
#     will stop new ones arriving — which matches what this session looked like.
#     Worth its own investigation; it is not solvable in this file.
#
# A WARNING ABOUT THE RESOLVER RULE, learned here: "it answers on 53, so it goes
# in custom-ip-blocklist.txt" is not safe as stated. A RESOLVER ANSWERS DNS; A
# TUNNEL MERELY USES THE PORT. The test is the traffic, not the port number —
# a resolver returns decodable responses to small readable queries, where this
# returns nothing decodable to large opaque datagrams. Eleven addresses in
# today's capture use port 53 this way and every one belongs in THIS file.
#
# --- The selected node -----------------------------------------------------
#
# 174.138.102.192 — DigitalOcean, 174.138.100.0/22, no PTR. THE NODE THE CLIENT
# SELECTED, and the only address in this session confirmed carrying the bulk of
# a tunnelled session rather than being probed and dropped. Transport is SSH,
# both on tcp/22 and on a high port, server banner OpenSSH 8.7.
#
# The client side is the more useful detail: the SSH client identifies itself as
# PuTTY as well as OpenSSH, from a handset. That is the signature of the Android
# SSH-tunnel apps (HTTP Injector, Dark Tunnel and relatives), which embed an SSH
# client and a candidate list, and it names the tool category behind most of
# this file. Seventh 174.138 address; first in this /22.
174.138.102.192

# UDP to a single peer is the signature of a CALL as much as a tunnel, and this

# tunnel. What tips it is the company it keeps — same session, same candidate

# traffic shape on its own. Recorded as the weaker of the eight.

# FIFTY-FIVE MORE — batch sixteen, 2026-08-13. Thirty-one handed over (two of
# the thirty-four listed were already here, and one was listed twice) plus
# twenty-four more read out of a SECOND ROUND OF CAPTURES taken later the same
# morning. Session total two hundred and twenty-five.
#
# Requested as "block"; filed here as throttle after putting the choice to the
# user, who confirmed. Recorded because the word matters: dropping these gives
# the client the clean per-node failure that the 2026-08-13 shaper change exists
# to deny it, and with the control channel described below the pool refreshes
# faster than a blocklist can grow. If the cap-only shaper turns out not to hold
# a node once it is deployed and re-observed, moving this whole population to
# custom-ip-blocklist.txt is the fallback that was discussed.
#
# WHAT THE SECOND CAPTURE ROUND ADDS, beyond more addresses:
#
#   * THE POOL ROTATED COMPLETELY. Not one of the nodes carrying traffic in the
#     later captures appears in the earlier ones. The client did not fail over
#     to a neighbour; it moved to an entirely fresh set.
#   * IT NO LONGER PUTS EVERYTHING THROUGH ONE NODE. The earlier round had a
#     single selected node carrying almost all of it. The later round spreads
#     comparable volume across three or four at once. Whether that is a setting,
#     a different profile, or a response to nodes going bad cannot be told from
#     a capture — but it means "find the one big peer" is no longer sufficient
#     as a detection method here, and byte-ranking now needs the top several
#     rows read rather than the first.
#   * A SECOND AKAMAI-FRONTED NAME. Alongside the invented name seen before,
#     another unregistered name is presented to a different Akamai edge range
#     (23.44.201.0/24, PTRs under deploy.static.akamaitechnologies.com,
#     AS20940 — real Akamai, not a relay). Two fronted names on two Akamai
#     ranges makes the control-channel reading firmer, and it stays outside what
#     this file can touch.
#
# THREE ADDRESSES FROM THESE CAPTURES WERE LEFT OUT, and the first two are the
# more important entries in this block:
#
#   * 2.182.242.131 — Iran Telecommunication Company, and 89.43.134.40 — High
#     Speed For Internet Services, Syria. Both carry STUN AND DTLS: WebRTC
#     signalling plus media. THAT IS A CALL, on consumer ISPs in two countries,
#     and shaping either would degrade somebody's conversation. This is the
#     third and fourth time this session that a carrier address in a capture has
#     turned out to be call media rather than a tunnel — after an IranCell
#     address in the earlier round — and it is why carrier space gets the
#     protocol checked rather than the provider assumed.
#   * 79.127.134.129 — Datacamp/CDNEXT, PTR under datapacket.com. Four packets,
#     no completed handshake, nothing to characterise. DataPacket carries both
#     CDN edge and VPN exits, and on four packets there is no way to tell which;
#     the two BunnyCDN addresses refused in batch fifteen are the same company.
#     Left out as insufficient rather than cleared.
